## Page 1


Linear Regression with Unknown Truncation
Beyond Gaussian Features
Alexandros Kouridakis
Anay Mehrotra
UT Austin
Stanford University
alexkouridakis@utexas.edu
anaymehrotra1@gmail.com
Alkis Kalavasis
Constantine Caramanis
Yale University
UT Austin
alkis.kalavasis@yale.edu
constantine@utexas.edu
Abstract
In truncated linear regression, samples (𝑥, 𝑦) are shown only when the outcome 𝑦falls inside
a certain survival set 𝑆★and the goal is to estimate the unknown 𝑑-dimensional regressor 𝑤★.
This problem has a long history of study in Statistics and Machine Learning going back to the
works of [Gal97; Tob58] and more recently in, e.g., [DGTZ19; DSYZ21; LWZ23; LMZ24]. Despite
this long history, however, most prior works are limited to the special case where 𝑆★is precisely
known. The more practically relevant case, where 𝑆★is unknown and must be learned from
data, remains open: indeed, here the only available algorithms require strong assumptions on
the distribution of the feature vectors (e.g., Gaussianity) and, even then, have a 𝑑poly(1/𝜀) run time
for achieving 𝜀accuracy.
In this work, we give the first algorithm for truncated linear regression with unknown survival
set that runs in poly(𝑑/𝜀) time, by only requiring that the feature vectors are sub-Gaussian. Our
algorithm relies on a novel subroutine for efficiently learning unions of a bounded number of
intervals using access to positive examples (without any negative examples) under a certain
smoothness condition. This learning guarantee adds to the line of works on positive-only PAC
learning and may be of independent interest.

Introduction
Selection biases, that is, systematic omissions in data, arise in almost all domains, from the physical
sciences to econometrics and social network analysis. Indeed, as Lisa Gitelman argues, all data
exhibits selection biases and the term “raw data” is an oxymoron [Git13]. Among different types of
selection biases, truncation is one of the most ubiquitous and challenging: truncation arises when
samples are recorded only when they fall inside a “survival” region 𝑆★, and all other samples are
deleted, or truncated, from the data, e.g., [Tob58]. Truncation arises naturally across diverse domains:
from econometrics [HW76] and astronomy [Woo85] to medical and biological sciences [KM03],
engineering [JYZ20; MEP21], and the social sciences [Bre96]. The difficulty of truncation lies in the
fact that no samples are observed outside the survival region. Thus, any algorithm must extrapolate
from data observed within 𝑆★to the region outside it. Standard estimation procedures such as
ordinary least squares fail to handle this and produce biased estimates, e.g., [Tob58; Mad83; Coh91].
In this work, we revisit the classical problem of truncated linear regression, which has a rich history
of study in statistics [Coh91], machine learning (e.g., [DGTZ18; DGTZ19; Ple21; LWZ23; LMZ24]),
and related fields [Mad83]. In this problem, we observe samples of the form (𝑥, 𝑦) where 𝑥is a
𝑑-dimensional feature vector and 𝑦is a scalar outcome. These samples are generated as follows:
first, the feature vector 𝑥is drawn from a distribution D; then, the output 𝑦= 𝑥⊤𝑤★+ 𝜉is computed,
where 𝑤★is an unknown regression vector and 𝜉∼N (0, 1) is standard Gaussian noise. Unlike in
standard linear regression, we do not observe every sample (𝑥, 𝑦). Instead, there exists a set 𝑆★⊆R,
called the survival set, such that we observe (𝑥, 𝑦) if and only if 𝑦∈𝑆★; otherwise, the sample is hidden
from us. The observed dataset thus consists of i.i.d. draws from the distribution of (𝑥, 𝑦) conditioned
on 𝑦∈𝑆★, and the goal is to estimate 𝑤★from these truncated samples (see Definition 1).
To gain some intuition, consider the following example from labor economics: Hausman and
Wise [HW76] studied the effect of education and (different measures of) intelligence (which are
modeled as a feature vector 𝑥) on the earnings 𝑦in low-income populations, but discovered that
the dataset only included an observation (𝑥, 𝑦) when the annual income 𝑦lay below a prescribed
eligibility threshold 𝜏, leading to the truncation set 𝑆★= (−∞, 𝜏]. More broadly, the survival
sets arising in real-world datasets can be far more complicated than a single threshold on the
response. A representative example comes from modern astronomical surveys, where whether a
star of a certain luminosity 𝑦is observed is governed by a multi-stage pipeline that can truncate
different non-contiguous intervals of luminosities. In practice, the resulting truncation set reflects a
composition of instrument detection limits, survey design choices, and upstream catalog filters,
among other factors, together making 𝑆★complex and hard to know explicitly [Woo85; Tee97;
SDS22b; SDS22a] (See Appendix A for a concrete list of various sources of truncation in large-scale
astronomical surveys). Beyond direct applications in problems where truncation arises, algorithms
for truncated linear regression also have applications in other domains, including treatment effect
estimation in observational studies (see, e.g., [CKMM+25] and references therein).
Prior Work.
Classical works in statistics laid the groundwork for truncated linear regression
[Tob58; HW76; Coh91]. However, these works were restricted to simple survival sets and did not
come with computational efficiency guarantees in high-dimensional feature-regimes that regularly
4


## Page 5


arise in modern applications. Indeed, no computationally efficient algorithm was known in high
dimensions until the work of Daskalakis, Gouleakis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [DGTZ19], who
studied the special case where the truncation set 𝑆★is precisely known via a membership oracle.1
Under certain assumptions (which we discuss later in this section), they gave a poly(𝑑/𝜀)-time
algorithm to obtain an 𝜀-approximation of 𝑤★. Several subsequent works have continued the study
of truncated linear regression in this known-survival-set setting, e.g., [DRZ20; DSYZ21; LWZ23].
However, as the earlier examples illustrate, in many practical settings the survival set is not
known to the analyst [Zam22]. When 𝑆★is unknown, no efficient algorithms are known in high
dimensions. In fact, even getting finite sample complexity bounds is non-trivial: if we put no
assumptions on 𝑆★, the parameter estimation problem is information-theoretically impossible
[DGTZ18]. To ensure statistical tractability, one must assume that 𝑆★has bounded “complexity,” for
instance, that 𝑆★is a union of at most 𝑘intervals (note that as 𝑆★is a subset of R, it is always a union
of intervals.) This leads us to the following problem, which is the main focus of this paper.
Main Question. Is there an algorithm that learns 𝑤★∈R𝑑to 𝜀accuracy in poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) time in
truncated linear regression when 𝑆★is unknown and a union of (at most) 𝑘intervals?
Under this assumption, Kontonis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [KTZ19] established a sample complexity
bound of poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) but had an exponential-in-𝑑running time and, hence, were not qualitatively
faster than brute-force algorithms also running in exponential-in-𝑑time (see Section 1.1). The
recent work of Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] made progress by designing the first
faster-than-brute-force algorithm for this problem, but only applied to the stylized case where the
feature distribution D is Gaussian and its running time remains exponential, namely 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀).2 This
assumption of Gaussianity on the features is crucial for their algorithm to work, but it constitutes a
very strong requirement on the feature vectors, which does not hold in real-world regression data. 3
Our Contributions.
Our main contribution (Theorem 1.1) is a poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀)-time algorithm that,
given access to samples from the truncated regression model, outputs an 𝜀-accurate estimate of 𝑤★
without knowledge of 𝑆★. Our algorithm is computationally efficient and importantly operates
under a set of assumptions that are the same as, or much weaker than, those in prior works. Notably,
we do not require the feature-distribution D to have a parametric form, e.g., Gaussian. Instead, we
only need D to satisfy the significantly weaker requirement of sub-Gaussianity. Concretely, we
require the following assumptions.
Assumption 1 (Survival Probability). There is a known constant 𝛼∈(0, 1], s.t., E𝑥∼D[Pr[𝑦∈𝑆★|𝑥]] ≥𝛼.
Assumption 2 (Sub-Gaussianity and Boundedness). There are known 𝜎, 𝑅≥1, s.t., the feature distribu-
tion D is 𝜎-sub-Gaussian and regression parameter satisfies ∥𝑤★∥≤𝑅.
1A membership oracle for 𝑆★is a primitive that, given a point 𝑥, outputs Yes if 𝑥∈𝑆★and No otherwise.
2When 𝑑≫𝑘, 1/𝜀, it holds that 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀) ≪(𝑑/𝜀)𝑑.
3While Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] require Gaussianity and have an exponential-in-𝑑running time, their
approach is technically interesting as they are able to handle high-dimensional truncation sets, over both 𝑥and 𝑦, while
all prior work on truncated regression, including ours, focus on handling truncation on 𝑦; see Remark B.2.
5


## Page 6


Assumption 3 (Identifiability). Let Dobs be the distribution of 𝑥in the observed data (see Equation (1)).
There exists a constant 𝜌∈(0, 1) such that E𝑥∼Dobs[𝑥𝑥⊤] ⪰𝜌2 · 𝐼.
A few remarks about the assumptions are in order:
1. The first assumption ensures that the amount of truncation is not too aggressive; at least a
constant fraction of the points survive. This assumption is information-theoretically necessary:
indeed, as Pr[𝑦∈𝑆★] approaches 0, then an unbounded number of samples are required to
estimate 𝑤★(as proved by [DGTZ18]).
2. The second assumption is a mild and standard tail condition on the features.
In fact,
Assumption 2 is even weaker than the assumptions required by prior works studying the
easier version of truncated regression with known survival sets: they require D to have a
bounded support [DGTZ19; DSYZ21].
3. The third assumption is required to ensure that 𝑤★can be identified from data in the survival
region; it is also made by prior works, e.g., [DSYZ21]. We prove that it is necessary in
Appendix E. We also mention that since we have sample access to Dobs, we can estimate the
parameter 𝜌from samples.
In short, Assumptions 1 and 3 are necessary for the problem to be solvable by a sample-efficient
algorithm, while Assumption 2 is weaker compared to corresponding assumptions in prior work.
We are now ready to state our main result in more detail.
Theorem 1.1 (Informal; see Theorem 3.1). Assume Assumptions 1 to 3 hold with 𝛼, 𝜌= Ω(1), 𝜎= 𝑂(1),
𝑅= poly(𝑑), and 𝑆★is a union of at most 𝑘intervals (for known 𝑘). Then, there is an algorithm that given
𝑛= poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) i.i.d. samples from the truncated linear regression model, with probability 0.99, outputs an
estimate b𝑤satisfying ∥b𝑤−𝑤★∥2 ≤𝜀. The algorithm runs in time poly(𝑛).
Importantly, both the sample complexity and running time of our algorithms scale polynomially
in the relevant parameters 𝑑, 𝑘, 1/𝜀. Moreover, the success probability can be made 1 −𝛿for any
𝛿∈(0, 1). Since the algorithm only requires Assumptions 2 and 3 on distribution D, it extends to the
cases where D is non-Gaussian. Further, in the special case where D is Gaussian, the above result
improves the best running time (namely, 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀) by [LMZ24]) to polynomial in all parameters of
interest (see Theorem 3.1).
Technical Overview. Next, we briefly overview the main challenges in proving Theorem 1.1 and
how we overcome them, while comparing with the work of [LMZ24] which requires Gaussian
features. A more detailed overview appears in Section 3.
Approach with known 𝑆★. Without any truncation, the standard method for regression is ordinary
least squares (OLS). A principled way to derive OLS is to view it as a special case of minimizing
the negative log-likelihood (NLL). This viewpoint extends naturally to truncated linear regression:
existing algorithms for estimating 𝑤★with known survival set 𝑆★minimize the (population version
of the) NLL function L𝑆★(·) using, e.g., stochastic gradient descent (SGD) [DGTZ19; DSYZ21; LWZ23].
This approach works because the NLL satisfies two key properties:
6


## Page 7


P1. L𝑆★(·) is convex and 𝑤★is its unique minimizer.
P2. L𝑆★(·) is strongly convex near 𝑤★.
Convexity ensures that one can find b𝑤such that L𝑆★(b𝑤) ≈L𝑆★(𝑤★), and strong convexity then
implies b𝑤≈𝑤★. However, the NLL depends explicitly on 𝑆★, and hence new techniques are
required when 𝑆★is unknown.
Existing approach with unknown 𝑆★. A natural idea is to first learn an approximation 𝑆of 𝑆★and
then minimize L𝑆(·). However, L𝑆(·) is not even well-defined when 𝑆≠𝑆★, so this direct approach
fails. Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] bypass this issue by designing a generalized objective
eL𝑆(·) that remains well-defined for 𝑆≠𝑆★and approximately retains the good properties of the
NLL when D is Gaussian. Their algorithm works in two phases: first, (i) it learns an approximation
𝑆of 𝑆★in 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀) time; then, (ii) it minimizes eL𝑆(·) using SGD. Both phases (i) and (ii) require the
Gaussianity assumption on the covariates, and the first set learning part runs in super-polynomial
time. We examine these issues and discuss how to overcome them next.
Issue I: Properties and Optimization of eL𝑆(·) without Gaussianity. We begin by examining phase
(ii), and we will discuss phase (i) afterwards. Hence, let us assume that the learning algorithm
is given an approximation to the true set 𝑆≈𝑆★. Now, we have to understand how to show the
desired properties of NLL (phase (ii)) without Gaussianity. In particular, using the fact that 𝑥is
Gaussian, Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] prove that E𝑥[∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥)] ≈04 when 𝑤≈𝑤★
(which implies that 𝑤★is an approximate minimizer of eL𝑆(·)). The key technical ingredient for their
proof is to show that the gradient ∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥) evaluated at 𝑥is sub-exponential, which crucially uses
the Gaussianity of 𝑥.
To go beyond Gaussian features, we follow a different route. We prove two important properties:
for any 𝑤≈𝑤★, we show that (i) ∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥) ≈0 with probability 1 −𝑜(1) over the draw of 𝑥in the
observed data, and (ii) while ∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥) can be large (and unbounded) with probability 𝑜(1), we
show that by suitably post-processing the set 𝑆, we can ensure E[∥∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥)∥] ≤poly(𝑑); this is
one place where we utilize the sub-Gaussianity of 𝑥to ensure that this post-processing can be
performed while retaining the fact that 𝑆is a good approximation of 𝑆★. These two properties
together suffice to show that ∇eL𝑆(𝑤) ≈0 when 𝑤≈𝑤★(Lemma D.10), implying that 𝑤★is close to
the minimizer of eL𝑆(Lemma D.11).
Finally, we must still minimize eL𝑆(·) efficiently. Here, prior works perform SGD analysis on NLL
but require a stronger assumption on the survival probability. To this end, since our assumption on
survival probability (Assumption 1) is simpler than that in [DGTZ19], we have to perform a new,
more complicated analysis for SGD (see Section 3 for details).
Issue II: Efficiently learning 𝑆★from positive samples only. For the above phase (ii) to be useful,
we need to get an estimation for the true set 𝑆★(and in particular in time poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀)). Hence, our
goal is to efficiently find 𝑆⊆R such that Pr[1{𝑦∈𝑆★} ≠1{𝑦∈𝑆}] ≈0; the challenge is that we
4Here ∇eL𝑆(𝑤; 𝑥) corresponds to the gradient of the NLL evaluated at the single point 𝑥with guess vector 𝑤. Since we
study the population version of NLL, we take an expectation over 𝑥drawn from Dobs. For a formal derivation of the
gradient, we refer to Appendix D.2.1.

only observe samples 𝑦inside 𝑆★(i.e., only positive samples) and no samples outside 𝑆★(i.e., no
negative samples). In particular, if we denote by D𝑦the marginal on 𝑦, then the learner observes
points 𝑦from the distribution with density proportional to D𝑦(𝑦)1{𝑦∈𝑆★}. Most results on PAC
learning from positive examples are negative: without assumptions on the underlying distribution
D𝑦, even simple classes such as unions of 𝑘> 1 intervals are information-theoretically unlearnable
[Nat87; Shv90]. A standard way to circumvent this impossibility is to obtain auxiliary information
about the marginal distribution D𝑦of 𝑦, e.g., by finding (and using samples from) a distribution eD𝑦
that is “close” to it [Den98; LMZ26]; see Definition 2.
In the truncated linear regression setting, Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] show how
to construct eD𝑦when 𝑥is Gaussian; this relies heavily on the parametric form of the Gaussian
and even fails for other parametric distributions. Our main contribution here is to give a new way
to construct this auxiliary distribution eD𝑦(Lemma 3.5) by only using the sub-Gaussianity of 𝑥
(without having any specific parametric form) and appropriate upper and lower bounds on the tail
of the noise-distribution.
Finally, we mention that even with access to eD𝑦, we need to design an algorithm for learning
the set that runs in time poly(𝑑, 𝑘, 1/𝜀). Existing algorithms require constrained variants of empirical
risk minimization [LMZ26], which are often NP-hard in high dimensions. We show that the
constrained empirical risk minimization algorithm of Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ26]
can be implemented in poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) time for one-dimensional sets (Theorem 3.4), using a carefully
designed greedy procedure (see Algorithm 3).
1.1
Related Works
Truncated statistics has a long history of study in statistics and econometrics [Gal97; Pea02;
PL08; Lee14; Fis31; HD99; Ras12] and has attracted considerable recent interest in machine
learning [DGTZ18; KTZ19; DRZ20; FKT20; NP20; Ple21; DNS23; LWZ23; NPPV+23; TA23; DLNS24;
DKPZ24; GKK24; KZZ24; ZZ25; CP26]. We refer the reader to Maddala [Mad83] for foundational
works. Below, we focus on works most relevant to ours.
Truncated Linear Regression. Daskalakis, Gouleakis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [DGTZ19] give the
first polynomial-time algorithms for truncated linear regression with bounded features and a known
survival set under variants of Assumption 1; Daskalakis, Stefanou, Yao, and Zampetakis [DSYZ21]
later extended this to unknown noise variance. In contrast, we study the significantly harder case
where the survival set is unknown. Even for known 𝑆★, our results slightly generalize [DGTZ19] by
only requiring the feature distribution to be sub-Gaussian rather than bounded.5
For unknown 𝑆★, Kontonis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [KTZ19] gave an algorithm for Gaussian fea-
tures with poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) sample complexity but 𝑘· (𝑑/𝜀)𝑑running time.6 Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis
[LMZ24] improved the running time to 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀), still for Gaussian features. Theorem 3.1 improves
upon both works: we only assume sub-Gaussian features and achieve poly(𝑑𝑘/𝜀) running time.
5Daskalakis, Gouleakis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [DGTZ19] also work in the fixed-design setting. Our algorithms
extend to fixed design with known 𝑆★, but we focus on the i.i.d. setting as it is more natural with unknown 𝑆★.
6Kontonis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [KTZ19] also had a 𝑑poly(𝑘/𝜀)-time method, but this did not apply to truncated
linear regression; it was for the simpler task of truncated gaussian mean-estimation with covariance Σ ≈𝐼.
8


## Page 9


Learning from Positive Examples.
A key subroutine in our work is learning the survival set
from positive examples only. This problem dates back to Valiant [Val84] and was formalized
by Natarajan [Nat87], who characterized classes that can be properly PAC learned from positive
samples alone. Shvaytser [Shv90] and Kivinen [Kiv95] completed the characterization for improper
learners and bounded the sample complexity. These results are largely negative: even unions of
𝑘> 1 intervals in one dimension are information-theoretically unlearnable without additional
assumptions. Subsequent works have designed algorithms when some information about the data
distribution D is available: Denis [Den98] reduced the problem to agnostic PAC learning when D is
known; Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ24] relaxed this to knowing a distribution eD close
to D; and Lee, Mehrotra, and Zampetakis [LMZ26] further generalized this to mild smoothness
assumptions on D. However, most general algorithms in these works run in time 𝑑poly(1/𝜀) or slower.
Our work contributes to this line by giving an efficient algorithm to learn unions of 𝑘intervals in
one dimension when the data distribution satisfies a smoothness condition (Definition 2).
2

Definition 1 (Truncated Linear Regression Model). The truncated linear regression model is parameterized
by a distribution D on R𝑑, a survival set 𝑆★that is a measurable subset of the real line R (w.r.t. the Lebesgue
measure), and an unknown parameter 𝑤★∈R𝑑. A sample (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R from the model is generated as follows:
1. The feature 𝑥is sampled from D (independent of all other random variables)

3. If 𝑦∈𝑆★, the sample is returned and, otherwise, Step 1 is repeated.

Hence, our algorithm indeed achieves polynomial dependence on the key problem parameters,

only sub-Gaussianity (Assumption 2) along with the very mild Assumption 1 and the information-
theoretically necessary Assumption 3. The dependence on the parameters in the exponent is

As we discussed, in the simpler case where 𝑆★is known, the sample complexity required to learn
𝑤★is Θ(𝑑/𝜀2) [DGTZ19]. Our increased sample complexity is due to the fact that we must learn
11

𝑆★. Here, our algorithm has a dependence on 𝑑because, to estimate 𝑤★∈R𝑑to distance 𝜀, our
algorithm needs to learn 𝑆★to accuracy poly(𝜀/𝑑), for which we need poly(𝜀/𝑑) samples. For more
details, we refer the reader to the full proof in Appendix D, and specifically Theorems D.1 and D.2.
Outline of This Section. Our Algorithm 1 operates in two phases: Phase I approximately learns
𝑆★, and Phase II uses this approximation to estimate 𝑤★. Both phases are technically involved and
require various tools to analyze, as we mentioned in Section 1, and present in more detail in this
section: In Section 3.1, we give an efficient algorithm for PAC learning unions of intervals from
positive-only samples. Section 3.2 shows how we use this algorithm to learn 𝑆★. Finally, Section 3.3
describes our approach for learning 𝑤★given 𝑆★: we define a generalized negative log-likelihood
objective (which is different from the usual negative log-likelihood), establish its required properties,
and show how to optimize it to obtain an estimate for 𝑤★.
3.1
Positive PAC Learning Unions of Intervals
Next, we introduce PAC learning from positive samples only (henceforth, positive-only learning)
and give an efficient algorithm for learning unions of intervals in this model.
Positive-Only Learning.
In positive-only learning [Nat87], as in classical PAC learning, there
is a distribution D★over a feature space X and some concept class H containing the target
concept ℎ★. In contrast to standard PAC learning, here we are only given sample access to the
conditional distribution D★
+ B D★|ℎ★(𝑥)=1 of positive samples (a distribution over X) and not
given any information about the negative samples. Our goal is to output a hypothesis ˆℎwith
small misclassification error under D★, i.e., Pr𝑥∼D★( ˆℎ(𝑥) ≠ℎ★(𝑥)) ≤𝜀. Crucially, we aim for small
misclassification error on both positive and negative examples, even though we only have sample
access to positives.
Smoothed Positive-Only Learning. [LMZ26] develop a smoothed version of positive-only learning,
where, in addition to positive samples, the learner also has access to a distribution D over the
feature space which is “smooth” with respect to D★. Intuitively, samples from D help the learner
identify high-probability regions of the unconditional D★. In particular, [LMZ26] introduce the
following notion of generalized smoothness, which extends notions of smoothness studied by works
in smoothed online learning, e.g., [HRS20; HHSY22; BRS24; HRS24; Bla25].
Definition 2 (Smoothness; [LMZ26]). For any 𝑠∈(0, 1] and 𝑞≥1, and any two distributions D, D★
over the same space, we say that D is (𝑠, 𝑞)-smooth w.r.t. D★if, for any measurable set 𝑆:
D★(𝑆) ≤1
𝑠· D(𝑆)1/𝑞.

this setting. Under this smoothness assumption on D, [LMZ26] establish the following learning
guarantee.

The algorithm of Theorem 3.3 is given in Algorithm 2 for completeness. We stress that the algorithm
is only sample-efficient and does not come with any computational-efficiency guarantee. That
said, this result is surprising because it ensures high-accuracy w.r.t. the base distribution D★, even
though we do not have sample access to D★itself at training time. This framework thus provides a
way to extrapolate information about D★from only D★
+ and a smooth reference distribution D. The
connection to learning 𝑆★in truncated regression is now apparent: in that setting, we only observe
samples in 𝑆★(i.e., positive samples). We formalize this connection in Section 3.2.
Although Theorem 3.3 is sample-efficient, it is not computationally efficient, since it solves an
13


## Page 14


empirical-risk-minimization problem which is, typically, computationally hard. However, when H
consists of unions of at most 𝑘intervals in R, we prove that the above becomes computationally
tractable. We thus obtain a polynomial-time algorithm for learning unions of 𝑘intervals from
positive examples provided the distribution D★satisfies the smoothness requirement (Definition 2).
Formally, let S𝑘be the collection of all unions of at most 𝑘intervals in R, and define the
hypothesis class H𝑘B {1𝑆: 𝑆∈S𝑘}. Given sample access to D and D★
+ as defined above, we can

Thus, Theorem 3.4 shows that with sample access to D★
+ and to a distribution D which witnesses
the smoothness of D★, we can efficiently PAC learn any union of 𝑘intervals under D★. The proof
appears in Appendix D.1. This result holds independent of our other results on truncated linear
regression and can be of broader interest.
Overview of Set-Learning Algorithm. The algorithm of Theorem 3.4 (Algorithm 3) is simple.
After drawing 𝑛samples from D★
+ and D, we consider the 𝑛−1 intervals on the real line defined by
consecutive samples from D★
+. We count how many samples from D fall into each of these intervals,
and discard the
𝑘−1
(𝜀𝑠)𝑞intervals containing the most samples. Finally, we return the union of the
remaining intervals. Figure 2 illustrates this procedure on one example.
The intuition behind discarding the intervals with the most samples from D is as follows: if
there are many samples from D between two consecutive samples 𝑦1 < 𝑦2 from P, then w.h.p.
either (𝑦1, 𝑦2) lies outside the support of D★
+ (i.e., outside of the set we wish to learn) and should be
discarded, or D★
+ places little mass in that interval (otherwise we would have seen another sample
from D★
+ inside it), so discarding it incurs little learning error. To make this intuition rigorous, we
need a careful analysis of the above greedy process; see Appendix D.1.

3.2
Learning the Truncation Set 𝑆★
Having presented our result on positive-only learning of unions of intervals under smoothness, we
now return to the truncated regression setting and show how to efficiently learn the truncation
set 𝑆★. To learn 𝑆★(which is a union of 𝑘intervals), we use Algorithm 3 from Theorem 3.4. The
challenge in this setting is that we do not have access to an auxiliary distribution D a priori (in order
to apply the smoothness framework from the previous section). To overcome this, we show how to
construct this distribution in the truncated regression problem.
Concretely, we must define the base distribution D★under which we wish to learn 𝑆★, and
show how to obtain sample access to D★
+ = D★|𝑆★and to some distribution D smooth w.r.t. D★. The
natural base distribution D★is the untruncated distribution over R of the response 𝑦. Formally, for
any 𝑤∈R𝑑, consider the distribution over (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R where the marginal of 𝑥is Dobs (see
Equation (1)) and the conditional distribution of 𝑦| 𝑥is the untruncated Gaussian N(⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1).
Denote by D𝑦(𝑤) the (unconditional) marginal distribution of 𝑦. Our goal is to approximately learn
𝑆★under D𝑦(𝑤★), i.e. to find a set 𝑆⊆R such that
D𝑦(𝑤; 𝑆★△𝑆) B Pr𝑦∼D𝑦(𝑤★)
 𝑦∈𝑆★△𝑆 ≤𝜀.
(2)
Here, 𝐴△𝐵≔(𝐴\𝐵) ∪(𝐵\𝐴) denotes the set symmetric difference. To learn such a set 𝑆, we apply
Theorem 3.4. This requires access to positive samples from D𝑦(𝑤★), as well as samples from a
distribution smooth w.r.t. D𝑦(𝑤★). We can obtain positive samples directly from our truncated
regression model, however it is not clear how to construct the smooth distribution. If we knew 𝑤★,
then we could obtain a sample 𝑥∼Dobs from the truncated regression model and then generate
(the untruncated) 𝑦∼N(⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1) to sample exactly from D𝑦(𝑤★), but 𝑤★is of course unknown.
Key Lemma: Sub-Gaussianity Implies Smoothness. To obtain the smooth distribution, we rely
on the observation that, under our sub-Gaussianity assumption for 𝑥, for any parameter vector 𝑤
within constant distance of 𝑤★, the distribution D𝑦(𝑤) is smooth w.r.t. D𝑦(𝑤★).
Lemma 3.5 (Smoothness). Suppose that Assumptions 1 and 2 hold.

When 𝑆= 𝑆★, the perturbed NLL is equal to the negative log-likelihood. However, in general, it is
not and may lack the desirable properties of the negative log-likelihood.
In Appendix D.2, we prove that ℒ𝑆has the desired properties. While we defer the details to the
appendix, we highlight some key steps here. An important issue with the perturbed log-likelihood
ℒ𝑆is that its global minimizer is not 𝑤★, so there is no a priori guarantee that optimizing ℒ𝑆will
recover 𝑤★. Moreover, even if 𝑤★were the unique minimizer, unless ℒ𝑆is also strongly convex
around 𝑤★, optimizing ℒ𝑆in function value need not yield convergence to 𝑤★. To resolve both
issues, we do not optimize ℒ𝑆globally, but instead restrict optimization to an appropriately defined
projection set P such that
R1. 𝑤★∈P;

R3. ℒ𝑆is strongly convex over P.

3.3.2
Algorithmic Considerations for PSGD
The above results yield a projection set P such that approximately minimizing ℒ𝑆over P produces
an estimate of 𝑤★. To actually run PSGD and obtain this approximate minimizer, we need to satisfy
some algorithmic considerations. Namely, we must (C1) find an initial point in P for the first
PSGD iteration, (C2) efficiently project onto P at each step, and (C3) obtain an unbiased gradient
estimate of ∇ℒ𝑆at each iteration. For (C1), we use our warm start b𝑤from before. Ensuring (C2)
is straightforward since P is a ball with known center and radius. Satisfying (C3), however, is
challenging, and we discuss it further in Appendix D.2.5.
To circumvent this challenge, we allow our gradient estimate to be biased: in particular, given
a point 𝑤∈R𝑑, we implement a subroutine that returns 𝑔(𝑤) ∈R𝑑satisfying E [𝑔(𝑤) | 𝑤] =
∇ℒ𝑆(𝑤) + 𝑏, where 𝑏is a bias vector satisfying ∥𝑏∥≤poly(𝑑𝜀). In Appendix D.2.5, we show how to
implement this biased sampler. We bound the runtime in Corollary D.14, while in Lemma D.15 we
7Throughout this section, we hide any constants from Assumptions 1 to 3 in the big-O notation, to simplify the
overview and highlight dependencies on the dimension 𝑑and the set learning error 𝜀.
17


## Page 18


show that the bias satisfies ∥𝑏∥2 ≤e
𝑂(𝑑1/2𝜀1/4), so we can control it by appropriately setting 𝜀. We
also prove an upper bound on the second moment of the (biased) stochastic gradients (Lemma D.16),
necessary for the convergence of PSGD. Finally, we combine these results with an analysis of PSGD
with biased gradients to show that running PSGD with projection set P and our biased gradient
sampler yields an estimate of 𝑤★, as desired.
4
Simulations with Synthetic Data
We empirically evaluate the rate-of-convergence of our algorithm on synthetic data. The simulation
code can be found in this repository.
Setup. The feature distribution is a 10-dimensional mixture of five Gaussians, and the truncation
set is a union of five intervals.
Baselines. Our baseline is Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) (which does not account for any truncation).
We compare the performance of three algorithms:
• Projected Stochastic Gradient Descent (PSGD) on the MLE objective with misspecified truncation
set 𝑆(which is the algorithm by Daskalakis, Gouleakis, Tzamos, and Zampetakis [DGTZ19] and
accounts for truncation but is provided an incorrect set 𝑆≠𝑆★);
• PSGD on the MLE objective with the correct truncation set 𝑆★(which is the same as the previous
algorithm except that it is provided knowledge of 𝑆★);
• Our algorithm (Algorithm 5).
We note PSGD with 𝑆★is an idealized algorithm that knows 𝑆★a priori; we cannot actually
implement it in practice since 𝑆★is unknown, and we only include it for comparison.
Results. The results of our experiments are shown in Figure 3. As expected, we observe that blindly
applying OLS without accounting for truncation can give significantly biased estimates, and the
same is true if we attempt to run PSGD on the MLE objective with an incorrect truncation set. On
the other hand, our algorithm converges to the true 𝑤★, at essentially the same rate as the ideal
algorithm which uses PSGD on the true MLE objective and assumes the exact knowledge of the
truncation set 𝑆★(which is unavailable in practice).
5
Conclusion
We provide the first polynomial-time algorithm for truncated linear regression with an unknown
survival set (Theorem 3.1). Our algorithm (Algorithm 1) operates under the mild assumption
that the covariate vectors are sub-Gaussian, and has polynomial dependence on all key problem
parameters. An important ingredient in establishing this result is an efficient procedure for learning
unions of intervals from positive examples under an appropriate smoothness condition (Theorem 3.4),
which may be of independent interest.
Our results suggest several directions for future work. First, truncated regression beyond the
linear setting, such as Poisson and logistic regression, have been studied with known survival
18


## Page 19


Figure 3: Simulation Results. The plot shows the Euclidean distance of the PSGD iterates 𝑤𝑡to the
true parameter vector 𝑤★(note that OLS is not an iterative algorithm, so its error is simply plotted
as a constant line). The experiment is repeated 10 times, and the shaded regions around each line
show standard deviation around the mean Euclidean distance. Our algorithm (“Full Algorithm”)
converges to 𝑤★at almost the same rate as the ideal algorithm (PSGD with true 𝑆★) which has exact
knowledge of the truncation set 𝑆★and, hence, is not implementable in practice.
set [IZD20] and extending our techniques to unknown survival set variants of these is a natural next
step. Further, we expect that natural extensions of our interval-learning algorithm can learn richer
hypothesis classes in constant dimensions (𝑑= 𝑂(1)) with just positive examples under smoothness,
provided they are well-approximated by unions of axis-aligned hypercubes. Some such classes are
studied by Durvasula, Haghtalab, and Zampetakis [DHZ23] and even shown to include classes
with infinite VC dimension.

## Page 33


A
Complex Survival Sets in Astronomical Surveys
In this section, we illustrate that survival sets arising in real-world data can be highly nontrivial:
rather than being simple threshold rules, they are often induced by a layered sequence of instrument
constraints, engineering decisions, and scientific priorities, many of which interact in ways that are
difficult to formalize.
To make this concrete, we focus on astronomy, where modern large-scale surveys have become
foundational infrastructure for astrophysics. These surveys, such as the Sloan Digital Sky Survey
(SDSS), Gaia, and the Vera Rubin Observatory’s Legacy Survey of Space and Time (LSST), system-
atically measure properties of millions to billions of objects, enabling population-level studies of
stars and galaxies, precision tests of cosmological models, and the discovery of rare phenomena
[YAAA+00; PBBV+16; IKTA+19]. At the same time, the scale and complexity that make these
surveys powerful also make selection effects unavoidable, and careful statistical analysis requires
understanding (or approximating) the induced survival set.
Warm-up. We begin with a classical and widely studied selection effect that arises from physical
limits of instruments.
Example A.1 (Malmquist Bias). Malmquist bias is a selection effect in astronomical surveys
driven by flux limits: any instrument can only observe objects whose apparent brightness
𝑦exceeds a detection threshold, while dimmer objects remain unobserved [Mal22; Mal25].
This truncation can systematically bias downstream inferences if it is ignored, leading to
incorrect conclusions about stellar populations and distances [Tee97]. For instance, among
objects at a fixed true distance, the brightness cutoff removes faint members; consequently, the
observed sample over-represents intrinsically brighter objects, unless the selection mechanism
is explicitly accounted for (as we do in our algorithm).
Selection effects in large-scale survey pipelines. While Malmquist bias is already a nontrivial
form of truncation, real survey pipelines typically induce much richer survival sets.
Example A.2 (Truncation Biases in Large-Scale Astronomical Surveys). Large-scale astronom-
ical surveys form the backbone of modern astrophysics: they enable scientists to identify
promising regions to study, evaluate foundational theoretical models, and discover rare and
new phenomena (e.g., [YAAA+00; PBBV+16; IKTA+19]). However, their massive scale and
operational complexity inevitably introduce truncation biases that must be corrected to draw
valid scientific conclusions.
A representative case study is the Apache Point Observatory Galactic Evolution Experiment
(APOGEE) [MSFP+17], one of the most comprehensive stellar spectroscopic surveys conducted
to date. APOGEE collected high-resolution near-infrared spectra for over 146,000 red giant stars
33


## Page 34


using custom-built spectrographs. The survey’s scale—spanning multiple years, numerous
institutions, and extensive operational logistics—illustrates why truncation effects are inevitable:
each stage of the pipeline introduces constraints and trade-offs that shape which objects are
ultimately observed and how.
Survey pipelines introduce numerous truncation biases, both known and unknown. Some
arise from fundamental physical limitations (such as Malmquist bias), while others emerge
from practical engineering constraints and resource allocation decisions. The question is
therefore not whether truncation biases exist, but how to account for their combined effects on
the observed sample. Even within a single survey like APOGEE, truncation effects arise at
multiple levels [MSFP+17]. To illustrate their diversity and the resulting complexity of the
induced survival set, we group some common mechanisms by their underlying causes (see
[SDS22a] for further discussion):
1. Sampling-dependent biases arise from target truncation strategies. Surveys often partition
targets into cohorts with different observation priorities, leading to truncation of some
cohorts.
For example, APOGEE stratifies targets by apparent brightness (H-band
magnitude) and preferentially targets certain strata. Importantly, cohort definitions can
depend on modeling choices: APOGEE preferentially sampled red giants over dwarfs
using a “redness” criterion, but this criterion depends on the chosen de-reddening
model used to convert observed color 𝐶obs into intrinsic color 𝐶corr, making truncation
model-dependent [SDS22b].
2. Physical limitations of instruments impose unavoidable constraints. Beyond flux limits,
atmospheric absorption can prevent observation of certain objects, and instrument
design can induce truncation over sky position. For instance, APOGEE uses multi-fiber
spectrographs that route light from many targets through optical fibers to a separate
spectrograph for high-precision analysis [WHSM+19; OCo20]. Physical constraints create
an “exclusion zone” around each fiber that prevents placing another fiber nearby, which
systematically under-samples dense regions such as star clusters and the inner Galaxy
[SDS22b], truncating some complex set of outcomes 𝑦.
3. Cascading effects propagate biases from upstream catalogs to downstream surveys. Many
surveys begin with a “parent catalog” and inherit its selection criteria. For example,
galaxy surveys that start from the Uppsala General Catalogue inherit criteria that omit
small angular-size galaxies unless they exceed brightness thresholds [FCWG+95]. These
inherited filters, composed with survey-specific constraints, yield selection functions that
are effectively complex convolutions of multiple stages.
The consequences of ignoring truncation effects can be severe. A particularly striking
example comes from NASA’s Kepler mission: an incorrectly implemented veto in pipeline
version 9.2 created an unexpected period-dependent detection bias, substantially reducing
34


## Page 35


the detectability of planets with orbital periods exceeding 40 days relative to theoretical
expectations [CCBJ+16]. The flaw went undetected until extensive validation efforts, and its
discovery necessitated reprocessing the catalog and issuing cautionary guidance for analyses
performed on earlier releases [CCBJ+16].
Figure 4: Illustrations for Example A.2. A multi-fiber spectroscope from the APOGEE survey, where
the requirement of a minimum distance between fibers creates spatially dependent selection effects.
Image credit: [SDS22c].

B.2
Known Survival Set
If the survival set 𝑆★is actually known in advance, then we can skip the first phase of our learning
algorithm (i.e., approximately learning 𝑆★) and directly implement the second phase (i.e., PSGD
on the log-likelihood function) to estimate 𝑤★. Since set estimation is the more computationally
demanding part of our algorithm, in this case, we obtain significantly improved sample complexity
and running time:

Consequences of Sub-Gaussianity of D
In this section, we prove some concentration results that are required in many of our proofs.
Specifically, we show that if 𝑥∼D is a sub-Gaussian random vector (as in Assumption 2) then
𝑥∼Dobs is also a sub-Gaussian random vector, and furthermore, for (𝑥, 𝑦) drawn from our truncated
regression model, the random vector 𝑦· 𝑥is sub-exponential. We start by reviewing the definition
of the sub-Gaussian and sub-Exponential norm, as well as some standard facts about them.
Definition 3 (sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential norms). For any random variable 𝑋, its sub-Gaussian
norm is defined as
∥𝑋∥𝜓2 B inf

(3)
It is also standard that the sub-Gaussian (or sub-exponential) norm of a centered random variable
is at most a constant factor different from the same norm of the respective uncentered random
variable (see Lemma 2.7.8 and Equation 2.26 of [Ver18]). That is, there exist absolute constants
𝐶1, 𝐶2 such that, for any random variable 𝑋

(4)

Detailed Proof of Main Result (Theorem 3.1)
In this section, we will formally prove Theorem 3.1, which our main result for learning 𝑤★in our
truncated regression setting. We gave an overview of the proof in Section 3, but here we will
rigorously present all technical parts and intermediate lemmas needed to prove the theorem. To
begin with, we state the following definition that will be used repeatedly throughout the proofs.
43


## Page 44


Definition 4 (Survival Probability). Given a measurable 𝑆⊆R and two vectors 𝑥, 𝑤∈R𝑑, define the
survival probability of 𝑥and parameter 𝑤with respect to the survival set 𝑆as 𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤; 𝑆) B N (𝑆; ⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1).
We will often use the shorthand 𝑝𝑆(𝑥) ≔𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤★; 𝑆). With this definition, the observed distribution
of the features (see Equation (1)) can be rewritten as
Dobs(𝑥) B 𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤★; 𝑆★) · D(𝑥)
E𝑥∼D [𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤★; 𝑆★)] = 𝑝𝑆★(𝑥) · D(𝑥)
E𝑥∼D [𝑝𝑆★(𝑥)] .
(6)
Next, recall from our discussion in the main body that we are working under the following
assumptions, which we state again using our new notation.
Assumption 1 (Survival Probability). There is a known 𝛼∈(0, 1) such that E𝑥∼D[𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤★; 𝑆★)] ≥𝛼.
Assumption 2 (Sub-Gaussianity of 𝑥). There is a known 𝜎≥1 such that 𝑥∼D is 𝜎-sub-Gaussian. That
is, for any unit vector 𝑣, it holds that ∥⟨𝑥−ED 𝑥, 𝑣⟩∥𝜓2 ≤𝜎.
Assumption 3 (Identifiability). There is a constant 𝜌> 0 such that E𝑥∼Dobs [𝑥𝑥⊤] ⪰𝜌2 · 𝐼.
In Assumption 2 as given in the main body, we additionally assume that ∥𝑤★∥is bounded by a
known constant 𝑅. Also, in the statement of Theorem 3.1, we assume a constant upper bound on
∥ED 𝑥∥2. Moving forward, in order to keep dependencies clear, we will not assume any such bounds
and will instead explicitly write these norms. We will also often use the shorthand 𝜇≔ED 𝑥.
Hence, ∥𝜇∥2 and ∥𝑤★∥2 will appear repeatedly in our results, and of course if an upper bound
is known for either of them it can be substituted. Furthermore, we will also state the following
assumption, which is essentially redundant.
Assumption 4 (Moment Bounds). There exist known constants Λ, 𝑀≥0 such that, for any unit vector 𝑣,

𝑣that E𝑥∼D ⟨𝑥−𝜇, 𝑣⟩2 ≲𝜎2, and therefore E𝑥∼D ⟨𝑥, 𝑣⟩2 ≲E𝑥∼D ⟨𝑥−𝜇, 𝑣⟩2 + ⟨𝜇, 𝑣⟩2 ≲𝜎2 + ∥𝜇∥2

substituted by 𝑂(𝜎+ ∥𝜇∥2). The reason why we choose to explicitly define Λ and 𝑀is because (i)
they allow us to more concisely present many of our results and calculations in the proofs, and (ii)
some of our results do not require the full power of sub-Gaussianity, but continue to hold under
only the moment bounds given in Assumption 4. Hence, we will repeatedly use Λ and 𝑀in our
results, and the reader can substitute each of them with 𝑂(𝜎+ ∥𝜇∥2) if desired.
Finally, recall from our discussion in Section 3.2 that, in order to be able to approximately learn
the truncation set 𝑆★, we require a known upper bound 𝑘on the number of intervals it consists of.
It will be convenient for us to state this requirement as an additional assumption.
Assumption 5 (Union of Intervals). The survival set 𝑆★is a union of at most 𝑘intervals in R, for a known
integer 𝑘.

With all the preliminary discussion out of the way, we now turn to presenting the proof of Theorem 3.1.
Recall that our main algorithm is divided into two steps: first, we learn an approximation 𝑆≈𝑆★of
the truncation set, and then given this approximation we run PSGD on the perturbed log-likelihood
ℒ𝑆to obtain an estimate for 𝑤★. For each of these steps, we will state a theorem quantifying the
guarantees of our algorithm. For the set-learning step, we have the following.

efficient implementation of Algorithm 2 of [LMZ26] for the special case where our hypothesis
class contains unions of at most 𝑘intervals on the real line. Note that, by our definition for the
distribution D𝑦(𝑤) in Section 3.2, for any measurable 𝑇⊆R, we can write the mass D𝑦(𝑤;𝑇) that

and then draw 𝑦∼N(⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1). Hence, our objective of learning a set 𝑆such that D𝑦(𝑤; 𝑆★△𝑆) ≤𝜀

the sense that it also guarantees that the output set 𝑆is fully contained within a bounded radius
from the origin; this will be useful to us for technical reasons in our proofs.

In other words, if we choose our set learning error 𝜀to be sufficiently small in the first step of the
algorithm, then we can efficiently approximate 𝑤★to high accuracy. Combining Theorem D.1 and
Theorem D.2 immediately yields the desired Theorem 3.1, simply by first running the algorithm of
Theorem D.1 with error 𝜀as given in Theorem D.2, and then running the algorithm of Theorem D.2.
Hence, in the remainder of this section, we will present the proofs of these two intermediate
theorems.
D.1
Proof of Set Estimation Guarantees (Theorem D.1)
As we discussed in Section 3, our approach for learning the truncation set 𝑆★relies on (i) an efficient
algorithm (Algorithm 3) for positive-only learning unions of intervals (Theorem 3.4) and (ii) a
way to obtain a distribution which is smooth w.r.t. the untruncated distribution of the response
𝑦(Lemma 3.5). In this section, we first prove the correctness and efficiency of Algorithm 3 in
Appendix D.1.1, thus showing Theorem 3.4, and then we prove our key Lemma 3.5 for establishing
smoothness of a distribution in Appendix D.1.2. Afterwards, in Appendix D.1.3, we examine how
we can make use of a warm start 𝑤which is a constant distance away from 𝑤★, in order to actually
sample from such a smooth distribution. Finally, in Appendix D.1.4, we combine our previous
results with some additional analysis to establish our desired Theorem D.1.
D.1.1
Proof of Theorem 3.4 (Correctness of Set Learning Algorithm under Smoothness)
We begin by proving Theorem 3.4, which is our main result for positive-only learning of unions of
intervals under smoothness.
10In particular, we require knowledge of the endpoints of the ≤𝑘/𝜀intervals constituting 𝑆. A simple membership
oracle to 𝑆does not suffice to run the subroutine of Lemma D.12 later on.

Proof of Theorem 3.4. Consider running Algorithm 2 (whose guarantees are given in Theorem 3.3)
in this setting, and we will show that this is equivalent to running Algorithm 3. Hence, the
correctness of our learner follows from the correctness of Algorithm 2. We begin by giving an
efficient procedure for implementing the ERM step of Algorithm 2. At any iteration 𝑖of Algorithm 2,
let 𝑦(1) ≤. . . ≤𝑦(𝑛) be the (ordered) positive samples of 𝑃⊂R, and let 𝑄B 𝑈∩𝑆𝑖⊂R
be the unlabeled points that survive. Recall that our objective is to find a union of at most 𝑘
intervals which contain all points in 𝑃and as few points of 𝑄as possible. Towards this, for any
1 ≤𝑖≤𝑛−1, let 𝑄(𝑖) B 𝑄∩(𝑦(𝑖), 𝑦(𝑖+1)) be the points of 𝑄between 𝑦(𝑖) and 𝑦(𝑖+1), and further let
𝑄(0) B 𝑄∩(−∞, 𝑦(0)) and 𝑄(𝑛) B 𝑄∩(𝑦(𝑛), ∞), so that 𝑄= ∪𝑛
𝑖=0𝑄(𝑖).
We can assume w.l.o.g. that the optimal solution to our ERM problem is a union of 𝑘disjoint
intervals with endpoints in 𝑃. Indeed, if a solution contains an interval with endpoints not in 𝑃,
we can simply shrink the interval until we hit the closest points of 𝑃, and this maintains feasiblity
(i.e.,all points of 𝑃are still covered by the solution) while making the solution no worse (i.e.,we
can only cover fewer points of 𝑄). Also, if a solution contains fewer than 𝑘intervals, we can pick
any one of these intervals which covers at least 3 points of 𝑅11, and split it into two intervals by
removing a sub-interval of the form (𝑦(𝑖), 𝑦(𝑖+1)), again maintaining feasibility while making the
solution no worse.
Therefore, to optimally solve our ERM problem, it suffices to consider unions of 𝑘disjoint
intervals with endpoints in 𝑃, and we need to find the one which covers the least points of 𝑄. It is
clear that we can do this via the following greedy procedure: we start with the interval [𝑦(1), 𝑦(𝑛)],
and we remove the 𝑘−1 sub-intervals of the form (𝑦(𝑖), 𝑦(𝑖+1)) corresponding to the 𝑘−1 sets 𝑄(𝑖)
with the most points.
We now claim that, when our hypothesis class is H𝑘, Algorithm 2 can be implemented in a
single iteration, instead of requiring 1/(𝜀𝑠)𝑞iterations. To see this, let 𝑈(𝑖) B 𝑈∩(𝑦(𝑖), 𝑦(𝑖+1)) be the
unlabeled points of 𝑈which are between 𝑦(𝑖) and 𝑦(𝑖+1), and define the re-indexing 𝑗1, . . . , 𝑗𝑛−1 so

D.1.2
Proof of Lemma 3.5 (Smoothness Property in Truncated Linear Regression)
Next, we prove Lemma 3.5, which shows how to obtain the smooth distribution required by
Theorem D.1 in our truncated regresion setting. Recall from our discussion in Section 3.2 that, for
any 𝑤∈R we define the distribution D𝑦(𝑤) as follows: consider the distribution over (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R
where the marginal of 𝑥is the Dobs and the marginal of 𝑦conditioned on 𝑥is an untruncated
Gaussian N(⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1). Then D𝑦(𝑤) is the unconditional distribution of 𝑦∈R.
To show Lemma 3.5, we need to relate the mass D𝑦(𝑤★,𝑇) that D𝑦(𝑤★) places on some set 𝑇⊆R
to the mass D𝑦(𝑤,𝑇) that D𝑦(𝑤) places (for some 𝑤∈R𝑑) on the same set 𝑇. Concretely, we need
to ensure that there does not exist a set 𝑇such that D𝑦(𝑤★) places significantly more mass on 𝑇
compared to D𝑦(𝑤). At a high level, there are two reasons why this might not hold: (i) the mean of
D𝑦(𝑤) can be much further away from 𝑇compared to the mean of D𝑦(𝑤★), and (ii) the distribution
Dobs of the features 𝑥projected on 𝑤might be significantly different compared to projecting on
𝑤★. As we will see in the proof, the first issue is taken care of by upper and lower bounds on the
tail of the (Gaussian) noise (which ensure that D𝑦(𝑤,𝑇) will be lower bounded even if the mean
of D𝑦(𝑤★) is significantly shifted) , while the second is resolved by sub-Gaussianity of 𝑥(which
ensures concentration around the mean of Dobs in all directions).
Lemma 3.5 (Smoothness). Suppose that Assumptions 1 and 2 hold.
Let 𝑤∈R𝑑be such that

E𝑥∼Dobs [N(𝑇; ⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1] > 0:
D𝑦(𝑤★;𝑇) ≤(1/𝑠) · 

D.2
Proof of Parameter Estimation Guarantees (Theorem D.2)
Throughout this section, we will assume that we have computed an approximation 𝑆≈𝑆★satisfying
the guarantees of Theorem D.1 with some set learning error 𝜀, using the machinery of the previous
section. Given such an 𝑆, we will show how to obtain an estimate for the desired parameter vector
𝑤★, thus proving Theorem D.2. As we discussed in Section 3.3, our algorithm is PSGD on the
following perturbed negative log-likelihood objective:
ℒ𝑆(𝑤) B −

(Perturbed NLL)
In all that follows, we will drop the inner conditioning when it is clear from context. The reason
why the inner expectation is over N (⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆∩𝑆★) instead of N (⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆★) (from which our
samples 𝑦are actually drawn conditioned on 𝑥) is because, unless 𝑆★⊆𝑆, in the second case the
distribution would place mass in regions where the quantity inside the expectation is undefined.
Furthermore, note that the perturbed NLL is different from simply substituting 𝑆for 𝑆★in the
expression of the negative log-likelihood, i.e., it is different from the expression
−

.
The reason for this choice is that, to run our PSGD algorithm, we will need to implement a
gradient sampler for ℒ𝑆, and this will require us to approximately sample from the distribution
in the expression of ℒ𝑆. That is, we will need to approximately sample from the distribution
over (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R where the marginal of 𝑥is Dobs and the marginal of 𝑦conditioned on 𝑥is
N(⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆∩𝑆★), and we will see that this is indeed possible. However, if we instead used the
latter display for the expression of the perturbed likelihood, the marginal of 𝑦conditioned on 𝑥in
the distribution we wish to sample from would be N(⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆). Unless 𝑆⊆𝑆★, it is unclear how
we would be able to sample from the desired distribution in this case; indeed, we only have sample
access to the truncated regression model, and this model only places mass for 𝑦inside of 𝑆★.
As we discussed, in the general case, the perturbed log-likelihood is not a log-likelihood and,
hence, may not have any desirable typical properties of log-likelihood, such as a global minimum
at 𝑤★. Through the analysis in the following sections, we will establish that the perturbed NLL
indeed satisfies properties that allow us to optimize it and obtain an estimate for 𝑤★.
D.2.1
Gradient and Hessian of Perturbed NLL
To begin with, we give the expressions for the gradient and Hessian of the perturbed log-likelihood.
These expressions can be verified by direct computation, and we present the details in Appendix D.3.1.

ℒ𝑆is strongly convex everywhere in P, and so we can indeed run PSGD using this projection set in
order to minimize ℒ𝑆. However, recall that the minimizer of ℒ𝑆in P is not, in general, the desired
parameter vector 𝑤★. Hence, we now turn our attention to showing that this minimizer satisfies a
desirable “closeness” property with respect to 𝑤★.

Therefore, Lemma D.11 establishes that we can make 𝑤min be arbitrarily close to 𝑤★, by appropriately
picking the set learning accuracy 𝜀(of course, smaller values of 𝜀imply increased sample complexity
and runtime in Corollary D.3). As such, in order to find a parameter vector arbitrarily close to 𝑤★,
we can simply run PSGD on ℒ𝑆on P (over which ℒ𝑆is strongly convex) until we converge to the
minimizer 𝑤min.
Having established this result, we now turn to algorithmic considerations for PSGD.

D.2.5
Efficient Gradient Sampler
To run the PSGD algorithm we need, at each iteration, to produce a vector 𝑔(𝑤) such that
E [𝑔(𝑤)] = ∇ℒ𝑆(𝑤), where 𝑤∈R is the point we are considering at the current iteration. Recall
from Lemma D.5 that
∇ℒ𝑆(𝑤) =
E
𝑥∼Dobs

−
E
𝑦∼N(⟨𝑤★,𝑥⟩,1,𝑆★∩𝑆) [𝑦· 𝑥] +
E
𝑧∼N(⟨𝑤,𝑥⟩,1,𝑆) [𝑧· 𝑥]

.
Implementing an exact gradient sampler is challenging. For example, to sample from the distribution
of the first term, one approach would be to draw samples (𝑥, 𝑦) from our truncated regression
model, and then perform rejection sampling to ensure that 𝑦∈𝑆. This would indeed give the
correct marginal of 𝑦given 𝑥(i.e.,N (⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆★∩𝑆)), however the marginal of 𝑥would not be
Dobs, but a biased version of it. Furthermore, for the second term, one could first draw 𝑥∼Dobs
from the truncated regression model to ensure that the marginal of 𝑥is correct, and then use
rejection sampling to draw 𝑧∼N (⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆) conditionally on 𝑥. This would indeed give an
unbiased sample for the second term; however, because we have not assumed that every 𝑥in the
support of Dobs has at least constant survival probability 𝑝(𝑥, 𝑤, 𝑆), we would be unable to bound
the runtime of the rejection sampling procedure.
Therefore, instead of implementing an exact gradient sampler, we will instead focus on generating
approximate gradient samples 𝑔(𝑤) such that E [𝑔(𝑤)] = ∇ℒ𝑆(𝑤) + 𝑏, for some controlled bias vector
𝑏. We will make use of the following result.
Lemma D.12 (Lemma 13 of [DGTZ19]). Let 𝜇∈𝑅, and let 𝑆⊂𝑅be a union of 𝑘closed intervals,
such that N(𝑆; 𝜇, 1) ≥𝑎for some 𝑎> 0. Then, for any 𝜁> 0, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
poly(log(1/𝑎, 𝜁), 𝑘) and returns a sample from a distribution with support ⊆𝑆and TV distance from
N(𝜇, 1, 𝑆) at most 𝜁.
Our method for obtaining approximate gradient samples is given in Algorithm 4.
Algorithm 4 Approximate Gradient Sampler
Input: Parameter vector 𝑤∈R𝑑, set 𝑆⊆𝑅, accuracy parameter 𝜁
Oracles: Sample access to the truncated regression model
Draw independent (b𝑥, b𝑦), (e𝑥, e𝑦) from the truncated regression model
Obtain sample e𝑧by calling the algorithm of Lemma D.12 for 𝑆, 𝜇B ⟨𝑤, e𝑥⟩and 𝜁
return 𝑔(𝑤) B e𝑧· e𝑥−b𝑦· b𝑥
To give some intuition about our gradient sampler, let
1. 𝑃be a distribution over (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R such that the marginal of 𝑥is Dobs and the marginal
of 𝑦given 𝑥is N (⟨𝑤★, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆★∩𝑆). Note that 𝑃depends only on 𝑆(but not 𝑤).
2. 𝑄be a distribution over (𝑥, 𝑧) ∈R𝑑× R such that the marginal of 𝑥is Dobs and the marginal
of 𝑧given 𝑥is N (⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩, 1, 𝑆). Note that 𝑄depends both on 𝑆and 𝑤.
58


## Page 59


If we could sample from 𝑃and 𝑄, we would have an exact sampler for the desired gradient and we
would be done. Our sampler samples from two other distributions 𝑃′, 𝑄′ that serve as proxies to
𝑃, 𝑄. In more detail, let
1. 𝑃′ be the distribution on ( ˆ𝑥, ˆ𝑦), which corresponds to sampling from the true model with
parameters 𝑤★, 𝑆★. We prove that the total variation distance between 𝑃′ and 𝑃is small by
using the fact that 𝑆is a good approximation of 𝑆★.
2. 𝑄′ be the distribution on (e𝑥,e𝑧), which corresponds to drawing first 𝑥∼Dobs and then drawing
the outcome from the Gaussian with mean ⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩and variance 1 conditional on 𝑆, using the
algorithm of Lemma D.12. We prove that 𝑄′ is close in total variation distance to 𝑄, by relying
on the guarantees of Lemma D.12.
These two results (see Lemma D.27) allow us to control the bias of our gradient sampler.
To analyze our algorithm, we will first bound its runtime with high probability, and then we
will control the bias of its samples. Clearly, the runtime is dominated by the call to the algorithm of
Lemma D.12, and by the guarantee of the lemma it suffices to lower bound the survival probability
𝑝(e𝑥, 𝑤; 𝑆) of the sample e𝑥drawn by Algorithm 4. As we show in the following Lemma D.13,
this probability might be exponentially small, however this is not an issue since the algorithm in
Lemma D.12 runs in time polylogarithmic in the inverse survival probability.

As we will see shortly, the convergence guarantees of PSGD essentially generalize even when our
gradient samples have bounded bias, and so the above result suffices for our purposes. In order
to obtain convergence guarantees for the PSGD algorithm, the last property we require from our
gradient sampler is that the second moment of the (biased) stochastic gradients is bounded by a
constant at each step of the execution. This is established in the following Lemma D.16, whose
proof is given in Appendix D.3.5.

To convert this constant-probability guarantee into a high-probability one, it suffices to apply a
standard boosting technique, similar to [DGTZ18]. Namely, we run PSGD a total of 𝐾B 𝑂(log 1/𝛿)
independent times to obtain average iterates { ¯𝑤1, . . . , ¯𝑤𝐾}. The guarantee of Corollary D.18 along
with a Chernoff bound imply that, with probability at least 1 −𝛿, at least 3/5 of the points ¯𝑤𝑖
satisfy ∥¯𝑤𝑖−𝑤min∥2 ≤𝜁/3. Conditioned on this event, we can return any point ¯𝑤ℓsuch that
∥¯𝑤ℓ−¯𝑤𝑖∥2 ≤2𝜁/3 for at least 3/5 of the points ¯𝑤𝑖; by the triangle inequality and our conditioning
event, any point satisfying ∥¯𝑤𝑖−𝑤min∥2 ≤𝜁/3 also satisfies this property, and so a desired point ¯𝑤ℓ
always exists. We claim that this point ¯𝑤ℓsatisfies ∥¯𝑤ℓ−𝑤min∥2 ≤𝜁. Suppose otherwise; then the
triangle inequality gives us that, for any ¯𝑤𝑖, either ∥¯𝑤𝑖−𝑤min∥2 > 𝜁/3 or ∥¯𝑤ℓ−¯𝑤𝑖∥2 > 2𝜁/3. However,
this is a contradiction, since at most 2/5 of ¯𝑤𝑖can satisfy the first inequality (due to the conditioning
event) and also at most 2/5 of ¯𝑤𝑖can satisfy the second inequality by definition of ¯𝑤ℓ.
In the above procedure, we run a total of 𝑇· 𝑂(log 1/𝛿) iterations of PSGD, each of which requires
only a single sample from the truncated regression model in the call to the approximate gradient
sampler (Algorithm 4). By a union bound, the runtime guarantee of Corollary D.18 holds for each
of these iterations with probability at least 1 −𝑇· 𝑂(log 1/𝛿) · 𝜂, and so if 𝛿is bounded away from
1 we can pick 𝜂≔
𝛿

substituting the bound for 𝑇from Corollary D.18, our above discussion can be summarized in the
following statement.

Remark D.20 (Improving exponential dependencies). In the case where the truncation set 𝑆★is
known (hence 𝜀= 0), our strong convexity constant has exponential dependence on the survival
probablitity 𝛼and on the moment bounds 𝜌, 𝑀. This is different compared to [DGTZ19], who
achieve a polynomial dependence. The technical reason for this is that [DGTZ19] show strong
convexity over a carefully designed projection set, such that all 𝑤in that projection set satisfy a
useful algebraic property; our projection set P (see Corollary D.9) is much simpler, and only bounds
the distance ∥𝑤−𝑤★∥2 of 𝑤∈P. The reason for using this simpler projection set as opposed to
the one in [DGTZ19] is that we also correspondingly make a simpler assumption on the survival
probability (namely, Assumption 1) in contrast to their (arguably) more complicated assumption
which, in turn, also enabled them to obtain a better dependency on 𝜌and 𝑀. We believe that
replacing Assumption 3 with their assumption and P with their projection set in our analysis would
yield polynomial dependence on 𝜌and 𝑀.

D.3.5
Efficient Gradient Sampler
Next we prove desirable properties of our efficient (biased) gradient sampler, namely, bounds on its
runtime, bias, and on the second moment of its stochastic gradients.

E
Necessity of Assumption 3
In this section, we will give a simple example of a setting where Assumption 3 fails and recovering
𝑤★is impossible, even while the rest of our assumptions are satisfied. Intuitively, if there exists a
direction of R𝑑where we see almost no samples from the truncated regression model (i.e., if the
83


## Page 84


eigenvalues of E𝑥∼Dobs [𝑥𝑥⊤] are not bounded away from zero), then we cannot hope to recover the
component of 𝑤★along that direction.
To state our example, we begin with some notation. For 𝑖∈[𝑑], let 𝑥𝑖∈R𝑑be the 𝑖-th standard
unit vector of R𝑑, so that {𝑥𝑖}𝑖∈[𝑑] are the vertices of the simplex on R𝑑. Let D be the uniform
distribution over {𝑥𝑖}𝑖∈[𝑑], and let 𝑆★≔[−1, 1] be our truncation set in one dimension. Since D
has bounded support, it is immediate that Assumption 2 is satisfied, and clearly 𝑆★is a union
of a bounded number of intervals. For large 𝐵≫1, we consider two possible parameter vectors
𝑤(1) ≔𝐵· 𝑥1 and 𝑤(2) ≔−𝐵· 𝑥1. For 𝑗= 1, 2, let P(𝑗) denote the distribution over (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈R𝑑× R
induced by our truncated regression model (Definition 1) with base distribution D, survival set 𝑆★,
and parameter vector 𝑤(𝑗).
Our claim is that P(1) and P(2) have vanishingly small TV distance as 𝐵grows, and therefore,
even if we know a priori that the true parameter vector is either 𝑤(1) and 𝑤(2), we still cannot deduce
which one it actually is using only sample access to the truncated regression model. To see this, we
first compute the survival probability of each point 𝑥𝑖in the support of D. For 𝑖= 1, by symmetry
of the Gaussian distribution and of 𝑆★, the survival probability is the same under both 𝑤(1) and
𝑤(2), and it is given by
𝜁≔𝑝𝑆★(𝑥1) = N(𝐵, 1; [−1, 1]) ≤exp
